          40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 1                                  40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 2   40.260.150        Multifamily Residential Outdoor Recreation Amenity Area Standards
 3
 4   A.      Purpose.

 5       Availability of amenity areas is an important aspect of livability and benefits public health and
 6   well-being. Amenity area standards are intended to assure opportunities for both private relaxation
 7   and for community interaction among residents. These standards are intended to ensure that
 8   required amenity areas are functional in terms of location, area, and level of improvement.

 9   A B.        Applicability.

10          1.   This section applies to multifamily, transitional housing and permanent supportive housing
11          developments containing twelve (12) eighteen (18) or more residential dwelling units.

12          2.     This section applies to co-living housing containing fifty-six (56) or more sleeping units.

13   C.    Amount of Amenity Area Required. The required amount of amenity area for multifamily
14   developments is equal to five (5) percent of the developable area of the lot as calculated per Section
15   40.220.020.C.6.a.

16        1.  Private outdoor amenity areas may be used to meet up to fifty percent (50%) of the total
17        amenity area requirement.

18        2.   Parking areas, required setbacks, required site landscaping, and driveways do not qualify
19        as amenity areas.

20        3.    Land designated by covenant or public dedication to be permanently maintained in an
21        undeveloped state because the land is identified as sensitive due to the presence of steep slopes,
22        unstable land, historical or archaeological sites, wetlands and buffers, or other permanent
23        physical development limitations as may be determined by the responsible official do not
24        qualify as amenity areas.

25   B D.        Private Residential Outdoor Amenity Areas Standards.

26        1.     Each ground-level residential living unit shall have an outdoor private area (patio,
27        terrace porch, yard) containing at least forty-eight (48) square feet and a width of at least four
28        (4) feet. A balcony used for an entrance or exit shall be considered an open space only if it is for
29        the exclusive use of the dwelling unit in question and it contains at least forty-eight (48) square
30        feet and a width of at least four (4) feet.

31        1.   Dwelling units may include private outdoor amenity areas including patios, terraces,
32        decks, balconies, porches and yards.

33        2.    Private outdoor amenity areas shall be directly accessible from the interior of the
34        dwelling unit.

35        3.    Private outdoor amenity areas shall contain at least forty-eight (48) square feet of area
36        with a width of at least four (4) feet.




     August 5, 2026 Draft
                                                                                                             129
         Clark County Code                                                                           Page 2/3
         40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 1        2 4.    Private outdoor amenity areas for multifamily residential units shall be screened from view
 2        from common amenity areas, other residential dwelling units, abutting land uses, and public or
 3        private streets through the use of landscaping, fencing, wall, change of grade or other similar
 4        design element. to the extent practicable using the orientation and location of structures,
 5        windows, and private outdoor spaces, landscaping and screening, natural features such as
 6        topography and open space, and built features such as windowless walls; provided, an
 7        applicant is not required to reduce the otherwise permitted density of a proposed development
 8        or to increase the cost of a proposed development by more than five percent (5%) per unit to
 9        comply with these standards.

10   C E.   Shared Recreation Areas for Multifamily Residential Uses Common Outdoor Amenity
11   Area Standards.

12        1.  Usable recreation space shall be provided in residential development for the shared or
13        common use of all residents in the following amounts:

14             a.   Studio size up to and including two-bedroom units, two hundred (200) square feet
15             per unit; and

16             b.     Three (3) or more bedroom units, three hundred (300) square feet per unit.

17        1.    Common outdoor amenity areas shall be a minimum of one thousand (1,000) square feet
18        in area, and amenity areas shall have a minimum horizontal dimension of twenty (20) feet. The
19        required amenity area may be provided as multiple individual areas, each meeting these
20        dimensions.

21        2.    The required recreation space may be all outdoor space or part outdoor space and part
22        indoor space and all public or common space or part public and part common space; provided
23        all public and common outdoor recreation spaces shall be readily observable from residential
24        dwelling units and/or public or private streets to allow for surveillance that contributes to
25        greater public safety.

26        2.    Common outdoor amenity areas shall be abutted by dwelling units or common areas on
27        at least two sides that include windows and/or entrances facing onto the amenity areas to
28        enhance residents’ access to such spaces and to allow for surveillance that contributes to
29        greater public safety.

30        3.     Common outdoor amenity areas shall be accessible to all dwelling units, and connected to
31        the site via pedestrian paths. Any pedestrian paths that cross parking areas shall be
32        differentiated with distinct paving materials or other similar techniques.

33        3.    The boundaries of public areas, such as streets or public gathering places, semipublic
34        areas, such as transition areas between streets and dwelling units, and private outdoor areas
35        shall be clearly defined so that a person can readily determine where the public space ends and
36        the private space begins, such as by using one (1) or more of the following:

37             a.     A deck, patio, low wall, fence or other suitable structures;

38             b.     Landscaping, such as a hedge or draping vine on a trellis or arbor;

39             c.     A change in the texture of the path material;

40             d.     Signs; or



     August 5, 2026 Draft
                                                                                                          130
         Clark County Code                                                                            Page 3/3
         40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 1             e.      Substantial natural features, such as a drainageway or tree grove.

 2        4.        Common outdoor amenity areas shall be improved as follows:

 3             a.    At least fifty percent (50%) of a common outdoor amenity area shall be improved
 4             with elements that enhance the usability and livability of the space for residents, including
 5             at least one feature besides (1) or (2):

 6                      (1)    Site furnishings (benches, tables, pedestrian-scale lighting);

 7                      (2)    Picnic areas;

 8                      (3)    Gazebo or other similar structures for weather protection;

 9                      (4)    Patios or courtyards;

10                      (5)   Rooftop decks if improved with furnishings including seating and planters,
11                      and accessible to all residents;

12                      (6)    Gardens, including community garden spaces;

13                      (7)    Play structures;

14                      (8)    Art or water features;

15                      (9)    Swimming pools;

16                      (10)   Sports courts such as tennis, pickleball, basketball, or volleyball;

17                      (11)   Fitness equipment;

18                      (12) Fenced pet area with furnishings including garbage cans, up to 25% of total
19                      required amenity area;

20             b.    The remaining unimproved portions of a common outdoor amenity area shall be
21             landscaped with grass, ground cover, shrubs, and/or trees. Stormwater low impact
22             development features that are not fenced such as rain gardens and infiltration planters
23             may be integrated in outdoor common amenity areas provided they do not exceed twenty-
24             five percent (25%) of the total required amenity area.

25        5.    Indoor amenity areas may be provided and counted towards the total required amenity
26        area. Indoor amenity areas may include indoor recreation spaces, game rooms, club houses,
27        indoor swimming pools or fitness facilities if improved and accessible to all residents.

28   F.    Alternative Amenity Area Design. The responsible official may approve and condition an
29   alternative design for common amenity areas that depart from the standards of subsection E as
30   part of the site plan review application if the responsible official finds that the proposed design will
31   equally or better meet the intent of the standards in subsection E. The total amount of amenity area
32   required by subsection C may not be reduced under this subsection.

33

          40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 1                                   40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 2   40.260.160     Nursery Schools, Preschools, Kindergartens, Commercial Child Day Care Centers,
 3   and Family Day Care Providers
 4   A.     Nursery schools, preschools, kindergartens and commercial child day care centers shall comply
 5   with the following criteria:

 6        1.   Minimum site size shall be ten thousand (10,000) square feet, except, when a preschool,
 7        kindergarten or commercial child day care center is designed as a part of an integrated industrial,
 8        commercial or multifamily development, in which case the minimum lot size may be reduced by the
 9        review authority, provided all other applicable code requirements are met.

10        2.    Provide and maintain outdoor play areas with a minimum area of one hundred (100) square feet
11        per individual based upon total capacity.

12             a.    The outdoor play area requirement shall not apply to strictly “drop-in facilities” where the
13             individuals cared for are not on the premises for more than three (3) hours in a twenty-four (24)
14             hour period; provided, that the requirements of the Washington Administrative Code are met.

15             b.     Facilities with a capacity of forty (40) individuals or more, under the licensing authority of
16             the state Department of Social and Health Services (DSHS), and with an approved “shifting
17             schedule” for the use of outdoor play area by DSHS, may calculate the outdoor play area based
18             on one hundred (100) square feet per individual using the outdoor area at any one (1) time;
19             however, a minimum of four thousand (4,000) square feet of outdoor play area must be
20             provided.

21             c.    Facilities with a capacity of thirty-nine (39) or less, or which do not qualify with a
22             “shifting schedule” as stated above, may count up to fifty (50) square feet of dedicated indoor
23             play area per individual of capacity toward the outdoor play area requirements.

24        3.     The play area shall be abutting the indoor facility.

25        4.   A sight-obscuring fence of at least four (4) feet, but not more than six (6) feet in height, shall be
26        provided around the outdoor play area.

27        5.     Adequate off-street parking and loading space shall be provided pursuant to Chapter 40.340.

28        (Amended: Ord. 2008-06-02)

29   B.     Family day care facilities providers shall comply with the following criteria:

30        1.   When located in a resource, rural or residential zone (R1-5, R1-6, R1-7.5, R1-10, R1-20, R-12,
31        R-18, R-22, R-30, R-43, OR-15, OR-18, OR-22, OR-30, OR-43 R-24, R-36, R-50, OR-24, OR-36,
32        OR-50, R-5, R-10, R-20, FR-80, FR-40, AG-20, and AG-WL districts), no exterior structural or
33        decorative alteration which will alter the residential character of a residence is permitted.

34        2.     Adequate off-street parking and loading space shall be provided pursuant to Chapter 40.340.

35        3.    Two (2) nonresident or non-family member employees are permitted if located within a
36        resource, rural or residential zone.

37        4.    Signage shall be limited to one (1) sign, not to exceed two (2) square feet in area, for
38        identification purposes only.

          40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 1                                   40.260 SPECIAL USES AND STANDARDS


 2   40.260.260     Zero Lot Line Developments
 3   A.    Purpose.

 4       Zero lot line provisions are intended to provide flexibility for the placement of structures in new
 5   residential land divisions in order to increase usable yard area. Zero lot line developments are different
 6   from townhouse developments in that structures are not attached at property lines.

 7        (Amended: Ord. 2024-03-02)

 8   B.     Applicability.

 9       These provisions can be used as an alternative to standard side or rear setback requirements in the R1-
10   5, R1-6 and R1-7.5, R-12, OR-12, R-18, OR-18, R-22 and OR-22 zoning districts, including within a
11   compact lot development subject to Section 40.260.072, when the requirements of this section are applied
12   in conjunction with a land division application.

13        (Amended: Ord. 2024-03-02)

14   C.     Requirements.

15        1.     Preliminary plats using zero lot line provisions may not be approved without approval of a
16        submitted site plan. Both the site plan and preliminary plat shall be consistent with the standards of
17        this section and all other applicable requirements.

18        2.    Conditions may be imposed to ensure that development on the resultant lots is consistent with
19        the approved site plan.

20        3.    Building permits for structures may only be approved when consistent with the approved site
21        plan and land division.

22        4.   Developments meeting all requirements of this section are exempt from separate site plan
23        review under Section 40.520.040.

24        5.     Zero setbacks may be applied to a side or rear property line; provided, that the setback on the
25        lot abutting a zero setback lot line shall be at least ten (10) feet in the applicable single-family
26        residential districts, and eight (8) feet in the applicable residential or office residential district, except
27        that the setback shall be at least six (6) feet in a compact lot development in any residential district.

28        6.    Structures less than three (3) feet to a property line must meet fire resistive provisions of the
29        residential building code.

30        7.    A note shall be placed on the final plat, and covenants running with the land shall be approved
31        by the Prosecuting Attorney and recorded with the County Auditor to guarantee that the required
32        setbacks are kept perpetually free of structures.

33        8.    Easements for such purposes as maintenance, or for building projection encroachments beyond
34        a zero setback line, shall be shown on the plat.

35        (Amended: Ord. 2024-03-02)

36   D.     Figure 40.260.260-1.

    40.340 PARKING, LOADING AND CIRCULATION


                           40.340 PARKING, LOADING AND CIRCULATION


40.340.010 Parking and Loading Standards
A.    General.

     1.     Applicability. Except as otherwise provided by the UDC, required off-street parking and
     loading spaces shall be improved and maintained as set forth in this section for all uses in all zoning
     districts.

     2.    Timing. Parking and loading spaces required for a given use or development shall be provided
     consistent with the approved site plan before the county issues an occupancy permit or final
     inspection for the use or development in question.

     3.    Availability.

          a.    Parking spaces required for a given use or development shall be available for the parking
          of operable passenger automobiles of residents, customers, patrons and employees only, and
          shall not be used for the storage of vehicles or materials, or for the parking of trucks used in
          conducting the business or use.

          b.   Loading spaces required for a given use or development shall be available for loading and
          unloading of trucks and similar vehicles.

          c.    Required off-street parking spaces may not be used for loading or unloading unless the
          responsible official finds that loading and unloading in those spaces will occur during hours of
          the day when the spaces are not needed for parking.

     4.    Location of Parking and Loading Facilities.

          a.    Off-street parking spaces for dwellings shall be located on the same lot as the dwelling.

          b.    Off-street parking spaces for other uses shall be located on the same lot as the use or on
          another lot not more than three hundred (300) feet from the building or use they are intended to
          serve, measured in a straight line from the building; provided, where required parking for a use
          or development will be located on a lot other than the lot on which the use it serves is located,
          then, before the county issues a building permit for the use or development, the applicant shall
          submit an agreement executed by the owner of the lot where the off-site parking is proposed
          authorizing use of the lot for the proposed parking spaces for the use in question.

          c.    Loading spaces for a given use shall be situated on the same lot as the use it serves, except
          as provided for joint use of facilities.

          d.    Parking and loading spaces shall not be located in a required front setback, except in the
          case of single-family or duplex dwellings. Access drives and maneuvering areas for parking and
          loading spaces shall not be located in a required front setback, except to the minimum extent
          practicable for access to the site.

          e.   Parking spaces for residential development may be arranged in tandem, with two or
          more vehicles, one in front of or behind the others with a single means of ingress and
          egress, at the rate of one space for every twenty (20) linear feet with any necessary
          provisions for turning radius.




September 15, 2026 Draft
                                                                                                            141
                                                                                                            140
    Clark County Code                                                                                  Page 2/6
    40.340 PARKING, LOADING AND CIRCULATION


     5.   Joint Use of Facilities. The responsible official may authorize use of given off-street parking
     and/or loading spaces by two (2) or more uses if:

          a.   The responsible official finds the applicant has shown that the uses that share the parking
          and/or loading do not require that parking and/or loading at the same time; and

          b.     Where shared parking or loading for a use or development will be located on a lot other
          than the lot on which the use it serves is located, then, before the county issues a building permit
          for the uses or developments on one (1) lot that will be served by the shared parking and/or
          loading on another lot, the applicant shall submit an agreement executed by the owner of the off-
          site lot where parking and/or loading is proposed authorizing use of the lot for the proposed
          parking and/or loading spaces for the use or development in question subject to terms and
          conditions that assure parking and/or loading will be available for each use it serves as provided
          in Section 40.340.010(A)(5)(a).

     6.    Change or Expansion of a Use. A site plan that changes the use of an existing structure or lot
     shall provide off-street parking and loading for the new use as required by this section. A site plan
     that enlarges an existing structure or use shall provide for additional parking and loading based only
     on the parking and loading requirements of the expansion, except as otherwise provided in
     Chapter 40.530.

     7.    Lighting. Light fixtures in parking or loading areas shall be consistent with RCW 47.36.180 on
     public roadways and not cast significant light or glare off site on adjacent properties.

     8.    Surfacing. All parking and loading spaces and related access drives, maneuvering, and vehicle
     storage areas shall be paved to standards, including the use of permeable pavements and grass block
     pavers, as approved by the responsible official except as follows:

          a.     Driveways leading to parking and maneuvering areas for unoccupied utility and wireless
          communication facilities need not be paved, except as required by Section 40.350.030(B)(7)(c)
          (this still requires the first twenty (20) to twenty-five (25) feet of driveway to be paved so gravel
          does not enter the paved road);

          b.     Three (3) or fewer parking spaces serving unoccupied utility and wireless communication
          facilities need not be paved;

          c.    Transitional uses such as coffee and food stands approved under Section 40.260.055;

          d.     Driveways used only for fire access purposes;

          e.    Parking areas for uses that receive access from unpaved roads;

          f.   Reuse of existing legally placed parking areas in the rural area of the county; provided, that
          proposed new parking and required ADA parking spaces and ADA accessways are paved;

          g.     Agricultural stands and markets as allowed under Section 40.260.025;

          h.     Rural kennels as allowed under Section 40.260.110;

          i.    Wineries as allowed under Section 40.260.245; and

          j.  Use of existing legally placed parking areas for residential development, up to a
          maximum of six (6) parking spaces; and



September 15, 2026 Draft
                                                                                                            142
                                                                                                            141
    Clark County Code                                                                                 Page 3/6
    40.340 PARKING, LOADING AND CIRCULATION


           j k.      Other uses as approved by the responsible official.

     9.    Drainage. Stormwater drainage facilities for parking and loading spaces and related access
     drives and maneuvering areas shall comply with Chapter 40.386.

     10.        Wheel Stops and Curbs.

           a.    Parking and/or loading spaces on the perimeter of a parking lot or abutting interior
           landscaped areas or sidewalks shall include a wheel stop or curb at least four (4) inches high
           located three (3) feet back from the front of the parking and/or loading space. Include breaks in
           curbs, as necessary, to allow flow of stormwater to LID stormwater facilities.

           b.   The front three (3) feet of a parking space may be improved with a low-growing vegetated
           LID feature, instead of asphalt or concrete pavement. However, this area shall not be counted
           toward landscape or open space area requirements unless it is part of a LID storm-water feature.

           c.    The perimeter of a parking or loading area and access and maneuvering drives associated
           with them shall be improved with a curb, rail or equivalent so that vehicles do not extend over a
           property line, sidewalk or public or private street. Breaks must be provided along the perimeter
           feature to allow stormwater movement to LID stormwater features.

     11.    Maintenance of Parking and Loading Areas. Required parking and loading spaces and
     associated access and maneuvering drives shall be maintained in good repair at all times.

          40.350 TRANSPORTATION AND CIRCULATION


 1                               40.350 TRANSPORTATION AND CIRCULATION


 2   40.350.030       Street and Road Standards
 3   […]

 4   B.     Standards for Development Review.

 5        […]

 6        13.     Urban Neighborhood Traffic Management.

 7              a.   Purposes. Urban neighborhood traffic management is intended to manage traffic speeds
 8              within residential neighborhoods and to discourage external traffic cutting through residential
 9              neighborhoods.

10              b.   Applicability. The provisions of this section shall apply only to access roads within a
11              development in the R1-5, R1-6, R1-7.5, R1-10, R1-20, R-12, R-18, R-22, R-30, R-43, OR-15,
12              OR-18, OR-22, OR-30, OR-43 R-24, R-36, R-50, OR-24, OR-36, OR-50, CR-1, CR-2, or MX
13              zoning districts which meet one (1) of the following conditions:

14                 (1)   Projected average daily trip of greater than six hundred (600) and less than two
15                 thousand (2,000) motor vehicles and a sight distance in excess of six hundred (600) feet; or

16                 (2)   Determined by the County Engineer, not his or her designee, that traffic calming
17                 measures and/or traffic calming devices are warranted.

18                     The review authority may waive the requirements of this section for Type I and Type II
19                 applications where the conditions listed above will not occur.

20              c.     Standards and Requirements. If the condition in Section 40.350.030(B)(13)(b) occurs,
21              traffic calming measures and/or traffic calming devices shall be required:

22                 (1)     Traffic Calming Measures. Traffic calming measures, such as ìTî intersections, street
23                 trees, curvilinear streets, or entry treatments, shall be incorporated into the overall
24                 development design to manage traffic speeds.

25                 (2)    Traffic Calming Devices. Traffic calming devices, such as speed bumps/humps and
26                 the devices shown in the Standard Details Manual, or as approved by the review authority,
27                 shall be installed.

          40.520 PERMITS AND REVIEWS


 1                                         40.520 PERMITS AND REVIEWS


 2   40.520.020     Uses Subject to Review and Approval (R/A)
 3   A.    Purpose.

 4       Upon review of the responsible official, uses designated as permitted subject to review and approval
 5   (R/A) may be allowed in the various districts; provided, that the responsible official is of the opinion that
 6   such uses would be compatible with neighboring land uses.

 7        (Amended: Ord. 2018-10-02; Ord. 2024-03-02)

 8   B.     Review Procedures.

 9       Uses subject to review and approval (R/A) shall be reviewed through a Type II process; provided, that
10   the responsible official, at his or her discretion, may refer any proposal to the hearing examiner for review
11   and approval, or denial. Any uses approved under the provisions of this chapter, by either the responsible
12   official or the hearing examiner in public hearing, shall be compatible with adopted county land use
13   policies and goals.

14        (Amended: Ord. 2018-10-02; Ord. 2024-03-02)

15   C.     Approval Criteria – General.

16        Except for the uses listed in Section 40.520.020(D), in approving a use, the responsible official shall
17   first make a finding that all of the following conditions exist:

18        1.      The site of the proposed use is adequate in size and shape to accommodate the proposed use;

19        2.    All setbacks, spaces, walls and fences, parking, loading, landscaping, and other features
20        required by this title are provided;

21        3.      The proposed use is compatible with neighborhood land use;

22        4.    The site for the proposed use relates to streets and highways adequate in width and pavement
23        type to carry the quantity and kind of traffic generated by the proposed use;

24        5.    The proposed use will have no substantial adverse effect on abutting property or the permitted
25        use thereof; and

26        6.    In the case of residential uses, the housing density of the development is consistent with the
27        existing zoning densities, or the general plan, and that all other aspects of the development are
28        consistent with the public health, safety, and general welfare for the development and for adjacent
29        properties.

30        (Amended: Ord. 2018-10-02; Ord. 2024-03-02)

31   D.        Approval Criteria – Special Uses.

32       When the following uses are allowed subject to review and approval (R/A) the responsible official
33   shall review them subject to the applicable standards and criteria in Chapter 40.260:

34        1.      Bed and breakfast establishments (Section 40.260.050);

Permitted Housing Types


                          Permitted Housing Types
                          The following pages illustrate the various types of housing permitted in the
                          mixed-use district, including:
                          y Mid-Rise Housing (5- or 4-over-1)
                          y Low-Rise Housing (3-over-1)
                          y Walk-Up Apartments
                          y Townhouses
                          y Duplexes, Triplexes, Quadplexes
                          y Cottage Housing
                          y Detached Single-Family Housing
                          y Accessory Dwelling Units




16                                                                      MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                               152
                                                                                    Permitted Housing Types


Mid-Rise Housing (5- or 4-over-1)
Mid-rise buildings are likely to occur only in the more urbanized areas with high
property values. Construction typically requires underground parking, which is
expensive to build. Consequently, mid-rise construction is most desirable in
urbanized areas served by transit and in locations with special pedestrian and
visual amenities.

Key Applicable Standards
y No density limits if building incorporates non-residential uses on ground floor
y Maximum height: 72 feet
y Open space requirements per Section A.1
y Building use, location, and orientation requirements per Section A.2
y Parking garage standards per Section B.5
y Building design standards per Chapter D


                               Mid-Rise Example:
                                y 39,000 SF lot size
                                y 5 floors (4-over-1)
                                y 20,000 SF retail/office
                                y 140 parking spaces




Clark County Mixed-Use Design Standards                                                                     17
                                                                                                      153
Permitted Housing Types


                                                             Low-Rise Housing (3-over-1)
                          Like mid-rise buildings, low-rise construction requires some form of structured
                          parking, and thus requires higher rents to make it viable. While low-rise
                          structures are often similar in size to walk-up apartments, the in-structure parking
                          allows a greater density and frees up more land for open space and amenities.
                          The most economical form of low-rise housing includes parking that is tucked
                          under the units on the ground floor (not built underground).

                          Key Applicable Standards
                          y No density limits if building incorporates non-residential uses on ground floor
                          y Building may be single purpose residential if entire site meets mixed-use
                            requirement
                          y Single purpose residential buildings are limited to 43 dwelling units per acre
                          y Open space requirements per Section A.1
                          y Building use, location, and orientation requirements per Section A.2
                          y Parking garage standards per Section B.5
                          y Building design standards per Chapter D




                                          Low-Rise Example:
                                           y 18,000 SF lot size
                                           y 3-4 floors




18                                                                      MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                                 154
                                                                                    Permitted Housing Types


Walk-Up Apartments
Since walk-up apartments rely on surface parking, they’re typically the most
affordable type of multi-family development to build. Special care must be taken
to site and orient the buildings, parking areas, and open space in a way that
contributes to the larger mixed-use district environment.

Key Applicable Standards
y No density limits if building incorporates non-residential uses on ground floor
y Building may be single purpose residential if entire site meets mixed-use
  requirement
y Single purpose residential buildings are limited to 43 dwelling units per acre
y Open space requirements per Section A.1
y Building use, location, and orientation requirements per Section A.2
y Parking lot location and design standards per Section B.4
y Building design standards per Chapter D




                                              Walk-Up Apartment Example:
                                              y 16,200 SF lot size
                                              y 3 floors
                                              y 12 units
                                              y 1.83 parking spaces/unit




Clark County Mixed-Use Design Standards                                                                     19
                                                                                                      155
Permitted Housing Types


                                                                                                             Townhouses
                                                With increasing land costs, townhouses are becoming the “single-family home”
                                                                                                    This section has been deleted.
                                                for the new generation of first time home-buyers in the Pacific Northwest.
                                                Townhouses have also proven to be popular with empty nesters seeking smaller
                                                spaces and no yard work. The mixed-use development examples herein show
                                                how townhouses can successfully be integrated into a pedestrian-oriented
                                                mixed-use environment.

                                                Key Applicable Standards
                                                y Larger development site must meet mixed-use requirement
                                                y Larger residential development must be within density range of 18-43
                                                  dwelling units per acre

 Townhouse Example:                             y Open space requirements per Section A.1

 y 16,800 SF lot size              y 3 floors   y Building use, location, and orientation requirements per Section A.2
 y 1,800 SF dwelling units         y 10 units   y Parking garage standards per Section B.4
 y 2 private parking spaces/unit                y Building design standards per Chapter D




20                                                                                           MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                                                     156
                                                                                                Permitted Housing Types


Duplexes
Duplexes provide an alternative to detached single-family homes and can
successfully be integrated with single-family developments, particularly when
used on corner lots with each unit facing a separate street. (See photo at right
and site plan.)

Key Applicable Standards
 y Larger development site must meet mixed-use requirement

                                                                 Accessory Dwelling Units
                          Accessory dwelling units (ADU) are encouraged to be integrated in
                          developments that feature detached single-family homes. They are a good way
                          to provide affordable housing in a neighborhood setting and may be used to help
                          meet minimum density requirements. Accessory dwelling units may be attached
                          to a home (AADU) or detached (DADU).

 Section A:                                           A.1 Open Space
 Site Design                                          Intent
                                                       To create focal points and gathering spaces of interest for the surrounding
                                                        neighborhood.
                                                       To provide a variety of accessible and inviting pedestrian-oriented areas to
                                                        attract shoppers to commercial areas and enrich the pedestrian environment.
                                                       To ensure that districts have areas suitable for both passive and active
                                                        recreation by residents, workers, and visitors and that these areas are of
                                                        sufficient size for the intended activity and in convenient locations.
                                                       To create usable, accessible, and inviting open spaces for residents.
                                                       To create open spaces that enhance the residential setting.

                                                      Standards
Figure A-1. Public open space is a critical
                                                      A.1.1 All developments shall incorporate publicly accessible open space on-
element of successful mixed-use developments.
                                                            site. This could include a single open space or, for larger developments,
                                                            a variety of open spaces. Examples could include a “village green”,
 Reports by the Lincoln Institute of Land Policy,
 the Trust for Public Land, and the National Park
                                                            “pedestrian-oriented space(s)”, “parkway”, “trail corridor”, “garden area”,
 Service, have quantified how parks and trails in           or other types of spaces described in A.1.2. Specifically, applicants must
 communities can boost property values. For                 successfully demonstrate how the proposed publicly accessible open
 instance, studies cited in the Trust for Public            space meets all of the following criteria:
 Land's 1999 report Economic Benefits of Parks
 and Open Space show that homes bordering the
 12-mile Burke Gilman trail in Seattle, WA sold for
 6 percent more than other houses of comparable
 size, while the percentage of Denver residents
 who said they would pay more to live near a
 greenbelt or park rose from 16 percent to 48
 percent between 1980 and 1990. Similarly, a
 three-mile greenbelt around Lake Merritt, near
 Oakland’s city center, was found to add $41
 million to the surrounding property values.


26                                                                                                 MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                                                          161
                                                                                                           A. Site Design

     a. Is/are centralized and accessible. All applicable open spaces shall
        be physically and visually accessible from the adjacent street or major
        internal pedestrian route. Open spaces shall be in centralized
        locations that nearby residents, workers, and/or shoppers can use –
        rather than simply left-over or undevelopable space in locations where
        very little pedestrian traffic is anticipated. Locations integrated with
        transit stops, for instance, would be encouraged, as there is likely to
        be pedestrian traffic in the area. At least 50 percent of the open
        space shall be at street level.

     b. Is/are inviting. Inviting open spaces feature amenities and activities
        that encourage pedestrians to use and explore the space. It could be
        a fountain, sculpture, children’s play area, dog run, special              Figure A-2. Centralized and accessible open
        landscaping element, or even a comfortable place to sit and watch the                                           space.
        world go by. And to linger in an open space, they must be
        comfortable. For instance, a plaza space should receive ample
        sunlight—particularly at noon—have design elements that lend the
        space a “human scale,” including planter boxes and other landscaping
        elements, benches and other seating areas, and pedestrian-scaled
        lighting.




                                                                                           Figures A-3 and A-4.
                                                                                           Comfortable and inviting open
                                                                                           spaces.

A. Site Design

 Lighting Guidelines                           c. Is/are safe. Safe open spaces incorporate Crime Prevention
                                                  Through Environmental Design (CPTED) principles:

                                                  (1) Natural surveillance – which occurs when parks or plazas are
                                                      open to view by the public and neighbors. For example, a plaza
                                                      that features residential units with windows looking down on space
                                                      means that the space has good “eyes” on the park or plaza.
                                                  (2) Lighting that reflects the intended hours of operation.

                                                  (3) Landscaping and fencing. Avoid configurations that create
                                                      dangerous hiding spaces and minimize views.

                                                  (4) Entrances should be prominent, well lit, and highly visible from
                                                      inside and outside of the space.
 Use the 3:8 rule
 to maintain                                      (5) Maintenance. Open spaces shall utilize durable materials that will
 visibility

                                                      necessary, shall be designed and treated to deter graffiti. Use
                                                      and maintain landscape materials that reduce maintenance cost
                                                      and maintain visibility, where desired.

     b. Individual balconies may be used to meet up to 50 percent of the

             they could serve as a major amenity to the development.

             in the calculations for required open space unless they provide a visual

Clark County Mixed-Use Design Standards                                                                                               39
                                                                                                                                166
                                                                                 B. Vehicular Access and Parking


B.4 Parking
Intent
 To provide flexibility in how developments accommodate parking.
 To maintain active pedestrian environments along streets by placing parking
  lots primarily in back of buildings.
 To ensure safety of users of parking areas, increase convenience to
  businesses, and reduce the impact of parking lots wherever possible.
 To physically and visually integrate parking garages with other uses.
 To reduce the overall impact of parking garages when they are located in
  proximity to the designated pedestrian environment.

Standards
See Section A.2 (Building Location and Orientation) for standards involving
parking lot location.
B.4.1 Parking spaces shall provided consistent with 40.340.010(A)(5).
      Exceptions:

       a. Multi-family dwelling – studio unit: 1 space/dwelling unit.

       b. Assisted care facility, senior: 1 space/each 3 units.

       c. Senior housing: 1 space/dwelling unit

B.4.2 Tandem parking (one car behind the other) may be used for all housing
      types, provided the spaces are identified for the exclusive use of a
      designated dwelling unit.
B.4.3 On-street parking spaces adjacent to uses shall count towards off-street
      parking requirements.




Clark County Mixed-Use Design Standards                                                                         61
                                                                                                          167
B. Vehicular Access and Parking

                                               B.4.4 For non-residential uses, the maximum number of parking spaces to be
                                                     provided is limited to one hundred twenty-five percent (125%) of that
                                                     required in CCC 40.340.010(A)(5).

                                               B.4.5 Shared parking between and among uses is encouraged and shall be
                                                     permitted in accordance with CCC 40.340.010(A)(5).

                                               B.4.6 On designated pedestrian-oriented streets:

                                                      a. Parking shall be at the side and/or rear of a building, with the
                                                         exception of on-street parallel parking. No more than sixty (60) feet of
                                                         the street frontage measured parallel to the curb shall be occupied by
                                                         off-street parking and vehicular access.

                                                      b. On-street, parallel parking shall be required on both sides of the
                                                         street.

                                                      c. Parking lots shall be located to the side or rear of the building
                                               B.4.7 Parking lots shall not be located adjacent to intersections. Exceptions
                                                     may be granted by the responsible official where alternative design
                                                     treatments, such as special landscaping and architectural components
                                                     adjacent to the street corner, enhance the visual character of the street
                                                     and the pedestrian environment and where the project meets all other
                                                     applicable design standards and guidelines.
                                               B.4.8 Parking structures on designated pedestrian-oriented streets shall provide
                                                     space for ground-floor commercial uses along street frontages for a
                                                     minimum of 75 percent of the frontage width. The entire façade facing a
                                                     pedestrian-oriented street shall feature a pedestrian-oriented façade.
                                               B.4.9 Parking structures adjacent to non-pedestrian-oriented streets and not
                                                     featuring a pedestrian-oriented façade shall be set back at least 10 feet
                                                     from the sidewalk and feature landscaping between the sidewalk and the
                                                     structure. This shall include a combination of evergreen and deciduous
                                                     trees, shrubs, and groundcover. Alternative measures shall be
Figure B-11. A good example of a parking             considered, provided the treatment meets the Intent of the standards.
garage entrance for a mixed-use development.


62                                                                                           MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                                                    168
                                                                                      B. Vehicular Access and Parking

B.4.10 Parking garage entries shall be designed and sited to complement, not
       subordinate, the pedestrian entry. If possible, locate the parking entry
       away from the primary street, to either the side or rear of the building.
B.4.11 Parking within the building should be enclosed or screened through any
       combination of landscaping berms, walls, decorative grilles, or trellis work
       with landscaping.
B.4.12 Parking garages visible from a street shall be designed to be
       complementary with adjacent buildings. This can be accomplished by
       using similar building forms, materials, fenestration patterns, and/or
       details to enhance garages.
B.4.13 An unbroken series of garage doors is not permitted on any street
       frontage, including walls facing controlled-access highways and freeways.

                                                                                                          D. Building Design


D.2 Architectural Scale
Intent
 To reduce the scale of large buildings and add visual interest.

Standards
D.2.1 The maximum building height shall be 72 feet.

D.2.2 Non-residential and mixed-use buildings shall include at least three of the
      following modulation and/or articulation features along all facades
      containing the public building entries (alley facades are exempt) at
      intervals of no more than 40 feet for designated pedestrian-oriented
      streets and 70 feet for all other streets:
         y Repeating distinctive window patterns at intervals less than the
           articulation interval.
                                                                                       Figure D-7. Example of building articulation.
         y Horizontal building modulation (depth at least 2 feet, width at least 4
           feet, and preferably tied to roofline modulation).
         y A separate covered entry or separate weather protection feature for
           each articulation interval.
         y Change of roofline.
         y Change in building material or siding style (perhaps coordinated with
           a change in building color).
         y Lighting fixtures, trellis, tree, or other landscape feature within each
           interval.
         y Alternative methods that meet the Intent of the standards, used per
           the required intervals, as approved by the responsible official.



                                                                                      Figure D-8. This building utilizes a number of
                                                                                              methods to reduce its perceived bulk.




Clark County Mixed-Use Design Standards                                                                                           75
                                                                                                                            170
D. Building Design

                                          D.2.3 Nonresidential and mixed-use building rooflines visible from a public
                                                street, open space, or public parking area shall be varied by emphasizing
                                                dormers, chimneys, stepped roofs, gables, prominent cornice or fascia, or
                                                a broken or articulated roofline. The width of any continuous flat roofline
                                                should extend no more than 100 feet without modulation. Modulation
                                                should consist of either:
                                                  y A change in elevation of the visible roofline of at least 4 feet if the
                                                    particular roof segment is less than 50 feet wide and at least 8 feet if
                                                    the particular roof segment is greater than 50 feet in length.
                                                    Exception: The responsible official may reduce or eliminate these
                                                    requirements where other treatments are successfully used to meet
                                                    the Intent of the standards.
                                                  y A sloped or gabled roofline segment of at least 20 feet in width and no
                                                    less than 4 feet vertical in 12 feet horizontal.
Figure D-9. Roofline standards for non-           y A combination of the above.
residential and mixed-use buildings.
                                          D.2.4 Multifamily residential buildings shall include building modulation. New
                                                building facades visible from the public and private streets, common open

                                                balconies, bay windows, or other architectural elements. Building
                                                articulation shall be accomplished with design elements such as the

                                                 a. Horizontal building modulation. Specifically:

Section G:                              G.1 Subdivision Design and Site Layout
Single-Family/                          Intent
Duplex                                   To ensure that single-family developments are compact, pedestrian friendly,
                                          and contribute to the character the surrounding neighborhood.
Developments                             Create variety and interest in the appearance of residential streets.
                                         To integrate open space with single-family developments.
Note: Single-Faily uses are no longer    Protect significant features of the natural environment.
permitted in the Mixed-Use zone. This
section applies only to duplexes.       Standards
                                        G.1.1 Development of “Neighborhoods.” New detached single-family/duplex
                                              subdivisions shall be designed to integrate with the larger mixed-use
                                              development and with surrounding properties and neighborhoods.
                                              Subdivisions shall be designed so that individual, separately developed
                                              projects work together to create distinct neighborhoods, instead of
                                              disjointed or isolated enclaves. To accomplish this, such developments
                                              shall comply with the following standards:
                                                 y A.1.4 regarding open space.
                                                 y B.1.3 regarding cross circulation.
                                                 y B.2.2 regarding street design.
                                                 y G.2 regarding lot and building design.




92                                                                                      MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                                               173
                                                                                           G. Single-Family/Duplex Developments


G.1.2 Cul-de-sac streets. The use of cul-de-sac streets should be avoided
      wherever possible and shall be limited to 10 percent of total lane miles in
      a development unless the applicant can successfully demonstrate that an
      alternative circulation pattern is not feasible. If cul-de-sacs are
      necessary, the end of each cul-de-sac shall provide a pedestrian walkway
      and bikeway between private parcels to link with an adjacent cul-de-sac,
      street, and/or park, school, or open space area, as determined by the
      responsible official.

G.1.3 Alleys. The use of alleys is strongly encouraged to minimize the
      appearance of garages from the street. For developments with more than
      50 single-family dwelling units, at least 25 percent of the homes shall be
      served be alleys.




Figures G-1 and G-2. The use of alleys is strongly encouraged. Accommodating vehicular access in an alley allows for
more front yard landscaping elements and a generous front porch.




Clark County Mixed-Use Design Standards                                                                                         93
                                                                                                                          174
G. Single-Family/Duplex Developments


                                                   G.2 Lot and Building Design
                                                   Intent
                                                    To enhance the character of the street.
                                                    To enhanced pedestrian access and walking.
                                                    To encourage interaction among neighbors.
                                                    To minimize the impact of vehicular access on the streetscape.
                                                    To ensure that new homes are built to a scale that is appropriate for the size
                                                     of the lot.
                                                    To ensure privacy of residents and adjacent properties.
                                                    To provide usable open space in the rear yard for residents.
                                                    To provide flexibility where unique site conditions exist.

                                                   Standards
                                                   G.2.1 Setback requirements shall be as follows:
                                                          a. Minimum front yard: 10 feet.

                                                          b. Minimum street, side or rear: 5 feet.

                                                          c. Maximum front yard: 20 feet.

                                                   G.2.2 Maximum floor area ratio shall be 0.5.

                                                   G.2.3 Maximum building height shall be 35 feet.

                                                   G.2.4 Porches or other covered and nonhabitable entry features at least 4 feet
                                                         by 6 feet facing a public street are required for each single-family home
                                                         and individual duplex unit. Such entry features may project up to 6 feet
Figure G-3. A front porch or other covered entry         into the required front yard.
feature is required.




94                                                                                              MAKERS architecture and urban design
                                                                                                                                       175
                                                                                      G. Single-Family/Duplex Developments

G.2.5 Garages shall be set back a minimum of 18 feet from the designated front
      property line, except where the garage does not face the street. The
      garage face shall occupy no more than 50 percent of the ground-level
      façade facing the street.
G.2.6 Where lots front on a public street and where vehicular access is from the
      street, garages or carports shall be set back at least 5 feet more than any
      front wall of the dwelling unit facing the street (as measured from the front
      property line). Exceptions:
        y The roof, eaves, or canopy of garages or carports may project to align
          with the front wall of the dwelling unit.
        y Where garages face to the side or rear yard, they may be placed to
          align with the front wall of the dwelling unit, provided the garage
          includes a window facing the street so that it appears habitable.
       On corner lots, this standard shall only apply to the designated front yard.

G.2.7 Where lots abut an alley, the garage or off-street parking area shall take
      access from the alley, unless precluded by steep topography.                           Figure G-4. Design standards for garages.
G.2.8 Driveway standards:
       a. No more than one driveway per dwelling unit. (Separate driveways
          for ADUs are not permitted.)
       b. Driveways for individual lots 50 feet or wider may be up to 20 feet in
          width.
       c. Driveways for individual lots less than 50 feet wide may be up to 12
          feet in width. Tandem parking configurations may be used to
          accommodate larger garages.

G.4 Cottage Housing
Intent
 To provide a housing type that responds to changing household sizes and
  ages (e.g., retirees, small families, single person households).
 To encourage creation of more usable open space for residents of the
  development through flexibility in density and lot standards.
 To ensure that the overall size, including bulk and mass of cottage structures
  and cottage housing developments, remain smaller and incur less visual
  impact than standard sized single-family dwellings, particularly given the
  allowed intensity of cottage dwellings.
 To provide centrally located and functional common open space that fosters a
  sense of community and a sense of openness in cottage housing
  developments.
 To provide private area around the individual dwellings to enable diversity in
  landscape design and foster a sense of ownership.
 To ensure minimal visual impact from vehicular use and storage areas for
  residents of the cottage housing development as well as adjacent properties,
  and to maintain a single-family character along public streets.

Standards

G.4.2 Cottage housing developments shall contain a minimum of four and a
       maximum of 12 cottages located in a cluster to encourage a sense of
  This page has been deleted.
       community among the residents. A development site may contain more
       than one cottage housing development.
G.4.3 Common open space requirements:

       a. Shall abut at least 50 percent of the cottages in a cottage housing
          development

       b. Shall have cottages abutting on at least two sides.

       c. Cottages shall be oriented around and have the main entry from the
          common open space

       d. Cottages shall be within 60 feet walking distance of the common open
          space

G.4.4 Required private open space shall be adjacent to each dwelling unit, for
      the exclusive use of the cottage resident(s). The space shall be usable
      (not on a steep slope) and oriented toward the common open space as
      much as possible, with no dimension less than 10 feet.

G.4.5 Cottage facades facing the common open space or common pathway
      shall feature a roofed porch at least 80 square feet in size with a minimum
      dimension of 8 feet on any side.

G.4.6 Cottages located adjacent to a public street shall provide a covered entry
      feature (with a minimum dimension of 6 feet by 6 feet) facing the street.

Title 40 Appendix F Overlay District Form-Based Standards

        4 Overlay Standards
4.0 Introduction
This chapter provides key site design standards and land use provisions that are specific to the different Overlays
and the 78th Street Heritage Farm Master Plan site delineated in the Chapter 2 Regulatory Maps. Sections 4.1
through 4.5 herein provide standards and guidance on building placement (setbacks), internal access, internal
open space, parking, maximum building height, permitted uses, and density for each type of Overlay.


4.0.1 Elements of the Overlay Standards
The sketch below notes the types of site design and land use provisions addressed in this chapter. On the
following two pages are clarification as to how these provisions are applied and references to any other
applicable code sections herein.




                                             Parking
                                                                  Building Height
    Front yard setback




                                                                                        Density
        Permitted Uses


                  Internal Open Space                                                   Permitted
                                                                                        Housing types
                                                    Internal Connectivity

    Figure 4-1. An example of design elements addressed in the overlay standards.



MAKERS                    CCC Title 40 Appendix F           48        OVERLAY STANDARDS                CHAPTER 4


                                                                                                           183
4.0.2 About Building Placement
This refers to front, side, and rear setbacks for structures. Some maximum front setbacks are included as well.
Front setbacks shall be measured from the edge of any street right-of-way, street tract, street easement, or
driveway easement that provides access to the lot, including any separate pedestrian easement that may exist
between a street and the front setback line.

4.0.3 About Internal Connectivity
Some regulatory maps for Activity Centers and Transitional Areas contain thick gray lines and arrows, which
refer to areas where improved internal pedestrian and vehicular connectivity is required in conjunction with
new development activity. Refer to Section 5.3 (Internal Pedestrian Access) and Section 5.4 (Internal Vehicular
Access) for guidance on how to achieve the required internal connections.


4.0.4 About Internal Open Space
This refers to internal open space that may be required with new commercial and/or residential development.
These internal spaces are private open spaces which may or may not be accessible to the public. Public parks
are not specifically addressed in this document (they will be addressed during the preliminary plat process,
which will require compliance with the Vancouver-Clark Comprehensive Parks, Recreation, and Open Space
Plan. While this Chapter defines the minimum amount of open space required, the design of open spaces are
addressed in Section 5.2.


4.0.5 About Parking
This section refers to the location and design of parking areas. Below are descriptions of each:
 • Location and maximum frontage: Location refers to areas where the parking shall be located with respect
   to on site buildings. For example, Side/Rear means that parking shall be placed to the side or rear of
   buildings and not in front. Maximum Frontage refers to the maximum percentage of the total site frontage
   that parking and vehicular access areas may occupy. See Section 5.5.2 for clarification and departures to
   these standards.
 • Design: Refers to the considerations for parking lot design. Internal pedestrian access standards within
   parking lots are provided in Section 5.3.1. Driveway standards are provided in Section 5.4.2. Parking lot
   landscaping standards are provided in Section 8.3.3.


4.0.6 About Building Height
Maximum building height is measured by the number of stories above grade. A story is the space in a building
between the surface of any floor and the surface of the next floor above (or the ceiling if there is no floor above).


4.0.7 About Permitted Uses
The Overlay Standards herein are intended to supplement the use provisions of CCC SubTitle 40.2.




MAKERS                    CCC Title 40 Appendix F        49           OVERLAY STANDARDS                  CHAPTER 4


                                                                                                             184
4.0.8 About Density
Density refers to residential density, specifically, the number of dwelling units per acre. This Chapter indicates
whether there is a minimum and/or maximum density level for each overlay. Densities shall be calculated
based on the gross area of the site minus any existing public rights-of-way, private road easements, or street
tracts. In some overlays, the minimum and/or maximum density may be the same as set forth under current
zoning designations as noted in CCC Chapter 40.220.020.


4.0.9 About Permitted Housing Types
The standards herein note which housing types are permitted and prohibited in applicable Overlays. A table
is included for each overlay zone identifying nine basic housing types. The housing types with a check (p) are
permitted. All other housing types are prohibited except where otherwise noted in this code or per underlying
zoning (CCC Title 40). Standards for the design of these housing types are provided and/or referenced in
Chapter 7.

4.0.10 About Additional Stepbacks Exceptions
Per RCW 36.70A.815, the additional stepbacks in any Overlay for buildings over a specific height do not apply to
the following types of residential development, as defined in CCC 40.100.070:
- Affordable housing;
- New construction meeting passive house requirements;
- The retrofit of existing buildings meeting passive house requirements;
- The conversion of existing buildings to housing or mixed-use development that includes housing;
- Modular construction; or
- Mass timber construction.

            7 Housing Type Standards
7.0 Introduction
7.0.1 Purpose
The purpose of this chapter is to identify standards that are unique to specific types of housing and that are not
otherwise addressed in other chapters of this code. Not ALL housing types are included, however. Standards
for the design of other housing types not referenced in this chapter are addressed in other chapters of this
code; for example, Chapter 3 for frontage design, Chapter 4 for overlay standards, and Chapters 5, 6, and 8
for site, building, service area, and landscaping design for stacked flats and other housing types permitted by
the underlying zoning and qualify as multifamily housing.



Table 7-1. Reference table for housing type standards.
     Overlay
    Activity Center Permitted Housing Types




    Transitional Area Permitted Housing Types1




    Multifamily Permitted Housing Types




    Mixed-Residential Permitted Housing Types




    Single-Family Residential Permitted Housing Types




    78th Street Property Permitted Housing Types

     For Transitional areas, all housing types are only permitted when integrated in a mixed-use development, vertically or horizontally.



    MAKERS                       CCC Title 40 Appendix F            100           RESIDENTIAL TYPOLOGIES                     CHAPTER 7


                                                                                                                                  199
7.0.2 Applicability
Standards for each of the housing types described herein apply to all subject housing types developed
throughout the sub-area. Where there is a conflict between the standards herein and other standards in this
code, the standards herein shall apply.


7.0.3 Relationship with Other Code Chapters
Unless otherwise noted for specific housing types herein, the following standards are set forth in other chapters
in this document:
 • Permitted frontage types: See Chapter 3, Frontage Type Standards (also see Table 7-2 to the right)
 • Front setbacks and frontage options: See Chapter 3, Frontage Type Standards
 • Locations for permitted housing type: See Chapter 4, Overlay Standards
 • Density requirements: See Chapter 4, Overlay Standards
 • Height limits: See Chapter 4, Overlay Standards
 • Side and rear yard setbacks: See Chapter 4, Overlay Standards
 • Parking requirements: See Chapter 5, Site Design Standards
 • Service areas, mechanical equipment, lighting, landscaping and signage: See Chapter 8, General
   Provisions


7.0.4 Other Housing Types
Below is a partial list of other housing types that are not specified in this chapter, but with references to other
key code sections (in addition to Chapters 2, 3, 5, and 8 to which conformance are required for all uses) herein
that provide design requirements:
 • Boarding Houses – Subject to Single Family Housing standards herein if in a single family context or
   subject to Chapter 6 (Building Design Toolbox) and Chapter 8 (General Provisions) if within a multifamily
   context, as determined by the Responsible Official.
 • Family Day Care Centers – Subject to Single Family Housing standards herein.
 • Retirement Housing Facility – Subject to Chapter 4 (Building Design Toolbox) and Chapter 8 (General
   Provisions).
 • Residential Care Homes - Subject to Single Family Housing standards herein.
 • Residential Care Facilities - Subject to Chapter 5 (Site Design Toolbox), Chapter 6 (Building Design
   Toolbox) and Chapter 8 (General Provisions).
 • Mobile Homes on individual lots - Subject to Single Family Housing standards herein.

7.1.1 Mid-Rise Apartments Description
Mid-rise apartments refer to five to six story buildings served by
elevators and structured parking (within or under the building).
These buildings are typically constructed with a concrete base
and four or five wood-framed floors above. They could be single-
purpose residential or mixed-use buildings with retail and/ or office
on one or more floors.
See Chapters 2, 3, and 4 for use provisions, permitted frontages,            Figure 9-1. This mid-rise apartment
                                                                             succeeds in following the set of building
and overlay district standards.                                              and site design standards. (Seattle, WA-
                                                                             Ballard neighborhood)

• 5.5.3 Parking Structure Design Standards
• 8.1 - 8.3 Service Area, Lighting, and Landscaping Standards

elevators. These buildings could be single-purpose residential or
mixed-use buildings with retail and/ or office on one or more floors.

and overlay district standards.

                                                                              Overlays where housing type is permitted
